People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Well, I guess what brings me in is... I just hardly eat right anymore. It's like, some days all I have is just coffee and maybe a piece of bread. I actually bought one last Tuesday and won five bucks, which ain't bad, but it ain't exactly gonna pay the rent, you know? But yeah, because I ain't eating, my pants are just hanging real loose on me lately. I actually had to punch a new hole in my belt with a screwdriver just to keep 'em up. I lost my good screwdriver though, left it sitting right on the bumper of my cousin's truck and he drove off... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, yeah, I just ain't got an appetite.

Voices? Nah, I don't hear nobody talking to me or nothing like that. It gets real quiet in my place, actually. Sometimes too quiet, you know? Well, unless you count the pipes. The pipes in my building rattle real bad whenever the guy upstairs takes a shower. He works some weird night shift, I think security or something down at the warehouse, so he's always showering at like three in the morning. Wakes me up all the time. I tried telling the landlord about the noise, but he don't care. He just sits in his little office smoking those cheap cigars all day. Smells awful, honestly, like burnt rubber or tires. I had a tire blow out on me once on the interstate, scared the living daylights out of me. Had to walk three miles in the pouring rain to get to a gas station. Ruined my favorite pair of boots, too. The water just soaked right through and the leather cracked down the middle. I loved those boots. But uh... nah, no voices in my head. Just the pipes.

Lately? Nah, not lately. But back then, I mean a while ago, I was sleeping maybe two or three hours a night and still jumping up in the morning like I had a full night's rest. Just completely good to go, you know? I'd jump right out of bed. Which is crazy 'cause my bed ain't exactly comfortable. I got the mattress from this thrift store over on 5th street. The lady there, she swore up and down it was barely used, but when I finally dragged it up the stairs to my place, I found this huge brown stain on the bottom of it. I really hope it was just coffee. 

I used to drink a ton of coffee, actually, especially when I was helping my uncle clear out his scrap yard a few years back. We hauled so much junk out of there. He had this old rusted-out Chevy Nova just sitting in the tall weeds out back. Beautiful car back in its day, but the engine was completely shot. We tried getting it to turn over for like three days straight before we just gave up and sold it for parts to this guy who showed up with a flatbed trailer. The guy had this really mean-looking bulldog sitting in the passenger seat of his rig, just staring at me the whole time we were loading the car. Gave me the creeps, honestly. Dogs with those squished-in faces, you never know what they're thinking. They always sound like they're choking on something. But uh... wait, what did you ask me? Sleep, right? Yeah, back then, two or three hours was all I needed and I was full of energy.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... right after I got fired from my job, I felt totally fired up. Like I was untouchable, you know? Just on top of the world. It was weird because getting fired usually bums people out, but I was just buzzing. I worked at this packaging plant down by the river. The smell from the river in the summer, man, it was awful. Like dead fish and old garbage cooking in the sun. They used to dump all kinds of chemicals in there back in the day, before all those environmental laws. My grandpa used to tell me stories about how the water would actually catch on fire sometimes. Can you imagine that? A river on fire. He was a fireman, actually, my grandpa. Had this big shiny brass helmet he kept on the mantle. I used to try it on when I was a kid, but it was way too heavy for my little head. Almost broke my nose once when it slipped down over my eyes. I still got a tiny scar right here on the bridge... but yeah, I felt untouchable back then. But if anybody tried to tell me to slow down or take it easy, I'd snap at 'em real fast. Just got so mad if they got in my way.

Unrealistic? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I was telling everyone I could start my own handyman business easy. Just like that, boom, start my own thing and show that packaging plant they made a huge mistake letting me go. I'm real good with my hands, always have been. The smell from that place was almost as bad as the river, smelled like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. He used to come home with his clothes smelling like it, and my mom would make him strip down on the back porch before he even came in the house. She was real strict about keeping the house clean. She used to scrub the linoleum floors on her hands and knees with a toothbrush. But yeah... I was telling people I was gonna run my own business and show 'em all. They thought I was crazy, but I knew I could do it.

I tried telling the landlord about the noise, but he don't care. He just sits in his little office smoking those cheap cigars all day. Smells awful, honestly, like burnt rubber or tires. I had a tire blow out on me once on the interstate, scared the living daylights out of me. Had to walk three miles in the pouring rain to get to a gas station. Ruined my favorite pair of boots, too. The water just soaked right through and the leather cracked down the middle. I loved those boots. But uh... nah, no voices in my head. Just the pipes.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been needing much less sleep than usual and still feeling full of energy?\n\nPatient: Lately? Nah, not lately. I'd jump right out of bed. Which is crazy 'cause my bed ain't exactly comfortable. I got the mattress from this thrift store over on 5th street. The lady there, she swore up and down it was barely used, but when I finally dragged it up the stairs to my place, I found this huge brown stain on the bottom of it. I really hope it was just coffee. \n\nI used to drink a ton of coffee, actually, especially when I was helping my uncle clear out his scrap yard a few years back. We hauled so much junk out of there. He had this old rusted-out Chevy Nova just sitting in the tall weeds out back. Beautiful car back in its day, but the engine was completely shot. We tried getting it to turn over for like three days straight before we just gave up and sold it for parts to this guy who showed up with a flatbed trailer. The guy had this really mean-looking bulldog sitting in the passenger seat of his rig, just staring at me the whole time we were loading the car. Gave me the creeps, honestly. Dogs with those squished-in faces, you never know what they're thinking. They always sound like they're choking on something. But uh... wait, what did you ask me? Sleep, right? Yeah, back then, two or three hours was all I needed and I was full of energy.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you only slept two or three hours, did you feel unusually happy or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... right after I got fired from my job, I felt totally fired up. Like I was untouchable, you know? Just on top of the world. It was weird because getting fired usually bums people out, but I was just buzzing. I worked at this packaging plant down by the river. The smell from the river in the summer, man, it was awful. Like dead fish and old garbage cooking in the sun. They used to dump all kinds of chemicals in there back in the day, before all those environmental laws. My grandpa used to tell me stories about how the water would actually catch on fire sometimes. Can you imagine that? A river on fire. He was a fireman, actually, my grandpa. Had this big shiny brass helmet he kept on the mantle. I used to try it on when I was a kid, but it was way too heavy for my little head. Almost broke my nose once when it slipped down over my eyes. I still got a tiny scar right here on the bridge... but yeah, I felt untouchable back then. But if anybody tried to tell me to slow down or take it easy, I'd snap at 'em real fast. Just got so mad if they got in my way.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did you have beliefs that other people said were unrealistic or not true?\n\nPatient: Unrealistic? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I was telling everyone I could start my own handyman business easy. When I was a kid, I used to take apart the toaster just to see how the coils got hot. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hospitalized? Nah, no way. I never went to the hospital for any of that. I mean, hospitals give me the creeps anyway. The lighting in those places, it's always that harsh fluorescent buzzing stuff. Makes everyone look like they've been dead for a week. I remember when my buddy Ricky broke his leg dirt biking out by the old quarry, we had to wait in the ER for like six hours. The chairs were bolted to the floor and they were made of this hard blue plastic that just dug right into your spine. Ricky was whining the whole time, but honestly, the guy sitting next to us was worse. He kept coughing into this crumpled up tissue that looked like it had been in his pocket since 1998. He had on these boots, too—those big heavy steel-toed ones, but the leather was all cracked and peeling like old paint. Reminded me of the paint on my front porch, actually. I've been meaning to scrape that down and repaint it before the winter hits, but you know how it goes. You buy the scraper, you buy the sandpaper, and then it just sits in the garage gathering dust. My garage is a mess right now anyway. But uh, yeah, no hospital for me. Never had to go in for feeling like that.

Yeah, I mean... yeah, definitely. There's been times, a lot of times actually, where it goes on for weeks. Most days I just wake up with this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest, you know? From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.9, F20.9, F06.8, F32.9, F33.9, F50.8, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brings me in is... I just hardly eat right anymore. It's like, some days all I have is just coffee and maybe a piece of bread. I usually get that cheap white bread from the corner store down the street from me. The guy who runs it, Jimmy, he's always trying to get me to buy those scratch-off lottery tickets every time I go in there. I actually bought one last Tuesday and won five bucks, which ain't bad, but it ain't exactly gonna pay the rent, you know? But yeah, because I ain't eating, my pants are just hanging real loose on me lately. I actually had to punch a new hole in my belt with a screwdriver just to keep 'em up. I lost my good screwdriver though, left it sitting right on the bumper of my cousin's truck and he drove off... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, yeah, I just ain't got an appetite.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Voices? Nah, I don't hear nobody talking to me or nothing like that. It gets real quiet in my place, actually. Sometimes too quiet, you know? Well, unless you count the pipes. The pipes in my building rattle real bad whenever the guy upstairs takes a shower. He works some weird night shift, I think security or something down at the warehouse, so he's always showering at like three in the morning. Wakes me up all the time. I tried telling the landlord about the noise, but he don't care. He just sits in his little office smoking those cheap cigars all day. Smells awful, honestly, like burnt rubber or tires. I had a tire blow out on me once on the interstate, scared the living daylights out of me. Had to walk three miles in the pouring rain to get to a gas station. Ruined my favorite pair of boots, too. The water just soaked right through and the leather cracked down the middle. I loved those boots. But uh... nah, no voices in my head. Just the pipes.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been needing much less sleep than usual and still feeling full of energy?\n\nPatient: Lately? Nah, not lately. But back then, I mean a while ago, I was sleeping maybe two or three hours a night and still jumping up in the morning like I had a full night's rest. Just completely good to go, you know? I'd jump right out of bed. Which is crazy 'cause my bed ain't exactly comfortable. I got the mattress from this thrift store over on 5th street. The lady there, she swore up and down it was barely used, but when I finally dragged it up the stairs to my place, I found this huge brown stain on the bottom of it. I really hope it was just coffee. \n\nI used to drink a ton of coffee, actually, especially when I was helping my uncle clear out his scrap yard a few years back. We hauled so much junk out of there. He had this old rusted-out Chevy Nova just sitting in the tall weeds out back. Beautiful car back in its day, but the engine was completely shot. We tried getting it to turn over for like three days straight before we just gave up and sold it for parts to this guy who showed up with a flatbed trailer. The guy had this really mean-looking bulldog sitting in the passenger seat of his rig, just staring at me the whole time we were loading the car. Gave me the creeps, honestly. Dogs with those squished-in faces, you never know what they're thinking. They always sound like they're choking on something. But uh... wait, what did you ask me? Sleep, right? Yeah, back then, two or three hours was all I needed and I was full of energy.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you only slept two or three hours, did you feel unusually happy or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... right after I got fired from my job, I felt totally fired up. Like I was untouchable, you know? Just on top of the world. It was weird because getting fired usually bums people out, but I was just buzzing. I worked at this packaging plant down by the river. The smell from the river in the summer, man, it was awful. Like dead fish and old garbage cooking in the sun. They used to dump all kinds of chemicals in there back in the day, before all those environmental laws. My grandpa used to tell me stories about how the water would actually catch on fire sometimes. Can you imagine that? A river on fire. He was a fireman, actually, my grandpa. Had this big shiny brass helmet he kept on the mantle. I used to try it on when I was a kid, but it was way too heavy for my little head. Almost broke my nose once when it slipped down over my eyes. I still got a tiny scar right here on the bridge... but yeah, I felt untouchable back then. But if anybody tried to tell me to slow down or take it easy, I'd snap at 'em real fast. Just got so mad if they got in my way.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did you have beliefs that other people said were unrealistic or not true?\n\nPatient: Unrealistic? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I was telling everyone I could start my own handyman business easy. Just like that, boom, start my own thing and show that packaging plant they made a huge mistake letting me go. I'm real good with my hands, always have been. When I was a kid, I used to take apart the toaster just to see how the coils got hot. My mom got so mad at me 'cause I couldn't always put it back together right, and one time it sparked and blew a fuse in the kitchen. We had to eat cold cereal in the dark for like two hours until my dad got home from the mill to fix it. He worked at the paper mill, you know? The smell from that place was almost as bad as the river, smelled like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. He used to come home with his clothes smelling like it, and my mom would make him strip down on the back porch before he even came in the house. She was real strict about keeping the house clean. She used to scrub the linoleum floors on her hands and knees with a toothbrush. But yeah... I was telling people I was gonna run my own business and show 'em all. They thought I was crazy, but I knew I could do it.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, were you ever hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? Nah, no way. I never went to the hospital for any of that. I mean, hospitals give me the creeps anyway. The lighting in those places, it's always that harsh fluorescent buzzing stuff. Makes everyone look like they've been dead for a week. I remember when my buddy Ricky broke his leg dirt biking out by the old quarry, we had to wait in the ER for like six hours. The chairs were bolted to the floor and they were made of this hard blue plastic that just dug right into your spine. Ricky was whining the whole time, but honestly, the guy sitting next to us was worse. He kept coughing into this crumpled up tissue that looked like it had been in his pocket since 1998. He had on these boots, too—those big heavy steel-toed ones, but the leather was all cracked and peeling like old paint. Reminded me of the paint on my front porch, actually. I've been meaning to scrape that down and repaint it before the winter hits, but you know how it goes. You buy the scraper, you buy the sandpaper, and then it just sits in the garage gathering dust. My garage is a mess right now anyway. But uh, yeah, no hospital for me. Never had to go in for feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yeah, definitely. There's been times, a lot of times actually, where it goes on for weeks. Most days I just wake up with this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest, you know? Like there's just nothing good waiting for me out there, so I just want to keep my head down and not deal with any of it. It's like waking up and feeling like you're already behind on everything. Which reminds me, I still haven't paid that water bill from last month. They sent me this pink notice in the mail, but the mailman around here is terrible. Half the time he puts my mail in Mrs. Gable's box next door. She's got this yappy little dog, a Pomeranian or something, that barks at literally everything. A leaf blows across the street and that dog loses its mind. She leaves it out in the yard all day while she goes to her knitting club or whatever she does. My buddy Ricky used to have a dog, a big Rottweiler mix named Buster. Buster was a good boy, but he chewed through the drywall in Ricky's kitchen once. Just ate a hole right through to the living room. Ricky tried to patch it himself with some plaster he found in his dad's shed, but it looked terrible. Looked like a giant lumpy tumor on the wall. But yeah... that heavy feeling just kind of sits there for weeks sometimes, making it hard to even care about getting out of bed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Serious problems? Nah, I wouldn't call it serious problems. I mean, sure, some people got a little annoyed with me, but they just didn't see the vision, you know? Like my buddy Ricky, he told me I was talking too fast and not making sense, but Ricky's always been slow on the uptake. I remember when we were trying to fix up his old Chevy Nova, the one with the rusted-out floorboards. We had to use old license plates to patch the holes so his feet wouldn't drag on the pavement. The guy at the junkyard charged us way too much for those plates, too. He had this glass eye that didn't quite move right, and he kept staring at me with his good eye while he counted the cash. I hate carrying cash, honestly. The coins always make my pockets heavy and they smell like dirty metal. My mom used to collect those state quarters when they first came out. She had this big cardboard map of the US and she'd pop the quarters in whenever she found a new one. I think she was only missing Hawaii when she gave up on it. But yeah, no serious problems, really. Just people not keeping up with me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yeah, definitely. I was going non-stop. I started calling up all these old tenants, making these huge lists, pricing out tools, and just trying to line up handyman work all day long. Way more than I ever did before, you know? I was looking at this one power drill online, a yellow and black one. Which is funny because it reminds me of those huge carpenter bees that used to get stuck in my grandma's screen door when I was a kid. We had this old wooden screen door that would slam so loud every time you let it go. Whack! Used to drive her absolutely crazy. She'd be in the kitchen making these little powdered sugar cookies... pecan sandies, I think she called them. They were so dry, man, you had to drink like a whole gallon of milk just to swallow one. Speaking of milk, they changed the caps on the plastic jugs at the corner store. They're attached to the plastic ring now so you can't drop them, but it just gets in the way when you try to pour it. Ends up splashing all over the counter and making a sticky mess. I hate sticky counters, you have to wipe them down like five times to get the residue off. But yeah, I was just making lists and pricing out tools all day.

Like, I was talking to the guy at the auto parts store the other day, just regular, asking about windshield wipers. The ones on my car are totally shot right now, they just smear the rain around instead of wiping it off. It's dangerous, honestly. Especially at night when the streetlights hit the glass. They changed all the streetlights on my block to those new LED ones, you know? They're so bright, man, it's like a spaceship beam coming right into my bedroom window. I had to put up aluminum foil just to get some sleep. My buddy Ricky did that once, but he used duct tape to hold it up and it left this awful sticky residue on the glass that he couldn't get off with anything. He tried scraping it with a razor blade and just scratched the whole window up. Looked terrible from the street. But yeah, no, usually people don't have a problem following me when I'm just normal.

When I was a kid, I used to take apart the toaster just to see how the coils got hot. We had to eat cold cereal in the dark for like two hours until my dad got home from the mill to fix it. He worked at the paper mill, you know? The smell from that place was almost as bad as the river, smelled like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. He used to come home with his clothes smelling like it, and my mom would make him strip down on the back porch before he even came in the house. She was real strict about keeping the house clean. She used to scrub the linoleum floors on her hands and knees with a toothbrush. But yeah... I was telling people I was gonna run my own business and show 'em all. I never went to the hospital for any of that. I mean, hospitals give me the creeps anyway. The lighting in those places, it's always that harsh fluorescent buzzing stuff. Makes everyone look like they've been dead for a week. I remember when my buddy Ricky broke his leg dirt biking out by the old quarry, we had to wait in the ER for like six hours. The chairs were bolted to the floor and they were made of this hard blue plastic that just dug right into your spine. Ricky was whining the whole time, but honestly, the guy sitting next to us was worse. He kept coughing into this crumpled up tissue that looked like it had been in his pocket since 1998. He had on these boots, too—those big heavy steel-toed ones, but the leather was all cracked and peeling like old paint. Reminded me of the paint on my front porch, actually. I've been meaning to scrape that down and repaint it before the winter hits, but you know how it goes. You buy the scraper, you buy the sandpaper, and then it just sits in the garage gathering dust. My garage is a mess right now anyway. But uh, yeah, no hospital for me. Like there's just nothing good waiting for me out there, so I just want to keep my head down and not deal with any of it. It's like waking up and feeling like you're already behind on everything. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really know, man. It's hard to say if it was a whole week or what, because time just gets all fuzzy when you're going like that. Like everything is just moving fast and blurring together. Kind of like when you're on the highway and the white lines on the road just turn into one long streak, you know? My cousin used to drive this beat-up old Chevy Nova, and the speedometer was completely busted, so we never actually knew how fast we were going. We'd just guess based on how loud the engine was screaming. That car was a total piece of junk, honestly. The heater only blew cold air, which was brutal in the winter. We used to have to wear our heavy winter coats inside the car just to drive to the store. I had this big puffy jacket, bright orange, like the ones hunters wear so they don't get shot in the woods. I never went hunting, though. I don't think I could shoot a deer, they just look at you with those big eyes, kind of like my neighbor's dog. It's a boxer mix, I think. Barks at every single person that walks by the chain-link fence, just going crazy over absolutely nothing. Drives me up the wall when I'm trying to think. But yeah, I couldn't tell you if it was exactly a week or not. I wasn't really looking at a calendar or anything.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.9, F20.9, F32.9, F33.9, F50.8, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brings me in is... I just hardly eat right anymore. It's like, some days all I have is just coffee and maybe a piece of bread. I usually get that cheap white bread from the corner store down the street from me. The guy who runs it, Jimmy, he's always trying to get me to buy those scratch-off lottery tickets every time I go in there. I actually bought one last Tuesday and won five bucks, which ain't bad, but it ain't exactly gonna pay the rent, you know? But yeah, because I ain't eating, my pants are just hanging real loose on me lately. I actually had to punch a new hole in my belt with a screwdriver just to keep 'em up. I lost my good screwdriver though, left it sitting right on the bumper of my cousin's truck and he drove off... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, yeah, I just ain't got an appetite.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Voices? Nah, I don't hear nobody talking to me or nothing like that. It gets real quiet in my place, actually. Sometimes too quiet, you know? Well, unless you count the pipes. The pipes in my building rattle real bad whenever the guy upstairs takes a shower. He works some weird night shift, I think security or something down at the warehouse, so he's always showering at like three in the morning. Wakes me up all the time. I tried telling the landlord about the noise, but he don't care. He just sits in his little office smoking those cheap cigars all day. Smells awful, honestly, like burnt rubber or tires. I had a tire blow out on me once on the interstate, scared the living daylights out of me. Had to walk three miles in the pouring rain to get to a gas station. Ruined my favorite pair of boots, too. The water just soaked right through and the leather cracked down the middle. I loved those boots. But uh... nah, no voices in my head. Just the pipes.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been needing much less sleep than usual and still feeling full of energy?\n\nPatient: Lately? Nah, not lately. But back then, I mean a while ago, I was sleeping maybe two or three hours a night and still jumping up in the morning like I had a full night's rest. Just completely good to go, you know? I'd jump right out of bed. Which is crazy 'cause my bed ain't exactly comfortable. I got the mattress from this thrift store over on 5th street. The lady there, she swore up and down it was barely used, but when I finally dragged it up the stairs to my place, I found this huge brown stain on the bottom of it. I really hope it was just coffee. \n\nI used to drink a ton of coffee, actually, especially when I was helping my uncle clear out his scrap yard a few years back. We hauled so much junk out of there. He had this old rusted-out Chevy Nova just sitting in the tall weeds out back. Beautiful car back in its day, but the engine was completely shot. We tried getting it to turn over for like three days straight before we just gave up and sold it for parts to this guy who showed up with a flatbed trailer. The guy had this really mean-looking bulldog sitting in the passenger seat of his rig, just staring at me the whole time we were loading the car. Gave me the creeps, honestly. Dogs with those squished-in faces, you never know what they're thinking. They always sound like they're choking on something. But uh... wait, what did you ask me? Sleep, right? Yeah, back then, two or three hours was all I needed and I was full of energy.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you only slept two or three hours, did you feel unusually happy or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... right after I got fired from my job, I felt totally fired up. Like I was untouchable, you know? Just on top of the world. It was weird because getting fired usually bums people out, but I was just buzzing. I worked at this packaging plant down by the river. The smell from the river in the summer, man, it was awful. Like dead fish and old garbage cooking in the sun. They used to dump all kinds of chemicals in there back in the day, before all those environmental laws. My grandpa used to tell me stories about how the water would actually catch on fire sometimes. Can you imagine that? A river on fire. He was a fireman, actually, my grandpa. Had this big shiny brass helmet he kept on the mantle. I used to try it on when I was a kid, but it was way too heavy for my little head. Almost broke my nose once when it slipped down over my eyes. I still got a tiny scar right here on the bridge... but yeah, I felt untouchable back then. But if anybody tried to tell me to slow down or take it easy, I'd snap at 'em real fast. Just got so mad if they got in my way.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did you have beliefs that other people said were unrealistic or not true?\n\nPatient: Unrealistic? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I was telling everyone I could start my own handyman business easy. Just like that, boom, start my own thing and show that packaging plant they made a huge mistake letting me go. I'm real good with my hands, always have been. When I was a kid, I used to take apart the toaster just to see how the coils got hot. My mom got so mad at me 'cause I couldn't always put it back together right, and one time it sparked and blew a fuse in the kitchen. We had to eat cold cereal in the dark for like two hours until my dad got home from the mill to fix it. He worked at the paper mill, you know? The smell from that place was almost as bad as the river, smelled like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. He used to come home with his clothes smelling like it, and my mom would make him strip down on the back porch before he even came in the house. She was real strict about keeping the house clean. She used to scrub the linoleum floors on her hands and knees with a toothbrush. But yeah... I was telling people I was gonna run my own business and show 'em all. They thought I was crazy, but I knew I could do it.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, were you ever hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? Nah, no way. I never went to the hospital for any of that. I mean, hospitals give me the creeps anyway. The lighting in those places, it's always that harsh fluorescent buzzing stuff. Makes everyone look like they've been dead for a week. I remember when my buddy Ricky broke his leg dirt biking out by the old quarry, we had to wait in the ER for like six hours. The chairs were bolted to the floor and they were made of this hard blue plastic that just dug right into your spine. Ricky was whining the whole time, but honestly, the guy sitting next to us was worse. He kept coughing into this crumpled up tissue that looked like it had been in his pocket since 1998. He had on these boots, too—those big heavy steel-toed ones, but the leather was all cracked and peeling like old paint. Reminded me of the paint on my front porch, actually. I've been meaning to scrape that down and repaint it before the winter hits, but you know how it goes. You buy the scraper, you buy the sandpaper, and then it just sits in the garage gathering dust. My garage is a mess right now anyway. But uh, yeah, no hospital for me. Never had to go in for feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yeah, definitely. There's been times, a lot of times actually, where it goes on for weeks. Most days I just wake up with this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest, you know? Like there's just nothing good waiting for me out there, so I just want to keep my head down and not deal with any of it. It's like waking up and feeling like you're already behind on everything. Which reminds me, I still haven't paid that water bill from last month. They sent me this pink notice in the mail, but the mailman around here is terrible. Half the time he puts my mail in Mrs. Gable's box next door. She's got this yappy little dog, a Pomeranian or something, that barks at literally everything. A leaf blows across the street and that dog loses its mind. She leaves it out in the yard all day while she goes to her knitting club or whatever she does. My buddy Ricky used to have a dog, a big Rottweiler mix named Buster. Buster was a good boy, but he chewed through the drywall in Ricky's kitchen once. Just ate a hole right through to the living room. Ricky tried to patch it himself with some plaster he found in his dad's shed, but it looked terrible. Looked like a giant lumpy tumor on the wall. But yeah... that heavy feeling just kind of sits there for weeks sometimes, making it hard to even care about getting out of bed.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did your behavior cause serious problems at work, with money, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Serious problems? Nah, I wouldn't call it serious problems. I mean, sure, some people got a little annoyed with me, but they just didn't see the vision, you know? Like my buddy Ricky, he told me I was talking too fast and not making sense, but Ricky's always been slow on the uptake. I remember when we were trying to fix up his old Chevy Nova, the one with the rusted-out floorboards. We had to use old license plates to patch the holes so his feet wouldn't drag on the pavement. The guy at the junkyard charged us way too much for those plates, too. He had this glass eye that didn't quite move right, and he kept staring at me with his good eye while he counted the cash. I hate carrying cash, honestly. The coins always make my pockets heavy and they smell like dirty metal. My mom used to collect those state quarters when they first came out. She had this big cardboard map of the US and she'd pop the quarters in whenever she found a new one. I think she was only missing Hawaii when she gave up on it. But yeah, no serious problems, really. Just people not keeping up with me.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. I was going non-stop. I started calling up all these old tenants, making these huge lists, pricing out tools, and just trying to line up handyman work all day long. Way more than I ever did before, you know? I was looking at this one power drill online, a yellow and black one. Which is funny because it reminds me of those huge carpenter bees that used to get stuck in my grandma's screen door when I was a kid. We had this old wooden screen door that would slam so loud every time you let it go. Whack! Used to drive her absolutely crazy. She'd be in the kitchen making these little powdered sugar cookies... pecan sandies, I think she called them. They were so dry, man, you had to drink like a whole gallon of milk just to swallow one. Speaking of milk, they changed the caps on the plastic jugs at the corner store. They're attached to the plastic ring now so you can't drop them, but it just gets in the way when you try to pour it. Ends up splashing all over the counter and making a sticky mess. I hate sticky counters, you have to wipe them down like five times to get the residue off. But yeah, I was just making lists and pricing out tools all day.\n\nDoctor: Even when you are not feeling especially up or depressed, do people still tell you that you talk in a way that jumps around or is hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Nah, I don't think so. When I'm just feeling regular, nobody says anything about how I talk. They understand me just fine. Like, I was talking to the guy at the auto parts store the other day, just regular, asking about windshield wipers. The ones on my car are totally shot right now, they just smear the rain around instead of wiping it off. It's dangerous, honestly. Especially at night when the streetlights hit the glass. They changed all the streetlights on my block to those new LED ones, you know? They're so bright, man, it's like a spaceship beam coming right into my bedroom window. I had to put up aluminum foil just to get some sleep. My buddy Ricky did that once, but he used duct tape to hold it up and it left this awful sticky residue on the glass that he couldn't get off with anything. He tried scraping it with a razor blade and just scratched the whole window up. Looked terrible from the street. But yeah, no, usually people don't have a problem following me when I'm just normal.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did you spend much more money than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really spend extra. I didn't have a lot of cash to throw around anyway, you know? I just keep what little I have in this old leather wallet I got at a flea market years ago. It's got one of those thick chains on it so nobody can swipe it. I like chains because I'm always losing my stuff, especially my keys. Man, just last week I spent like two hours looking for my house keys. Turned out they fell way down deep in the couch cushions. Have you ever actually pulled the cushions off and looked under there? It's crazy what gets stuck down in the cracks. I found this old remote control for a TV I haven't even owned since, like, 2015. It was one of those huge heavy box TVs that takes two guys to lift. When I moved out of my old place, Ricky helped me carry it down three flights of stairs and he almost dropped his end right on his toe. He was wearing these cheap rubber flip-flops, which is just a stupid idea when you're moving heavy furniture. I told him he should go down to the army surplus store and get some steel-toe boots. They have a whole wall of them, but the whole store smells like mothballs. I can't stand that smell, it gets stuck in your nose for the rest of the day and makes everything taste weird. But yeah, no, I wasn't buying a bunch of stuff or spending more money.\n\nDoctor: That time when you felt untouchable and needed only two or three hours of sleep—did that last for at least a week?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, man. It's hard to say if it was a whole week or what, because time just gets all fuzzy when you're going like that. Like everything is just moving fast and blurring together. Kind of like when you're on the highway and the white lines on the road just turn into one long streak, you know? My cousin used to drive this beat-up old Chevy Nova, and the speedometer was completely busted, so we never actually knew how fast we were going. We'd just guess based on how loud the engine was screaming. That car was a total piece of junk, honestly. The heater only blew cold air, which was brutal in the winter. We used to have to wear our heavy winter coats inside the car just to drive to the store. I had this big puffy jacket, bright orange, like the ones hunters wear so they don't get shot in the woods. I never went hunting, though. I don't think I could shoot a deer, they just look at you with those big eyes, kind of like my neighbor's dog. It's a boxer mix, I think. Barks at every single person that walks by the chain-link fence, just going crazy over absolutely nothing. Drives me up the wall when I'm trying to think. But yeah, I couldn't tell you if it was exactly a week or not. I wasn't really looking at a calendar or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, definitely, man. I was telling everyone I was gonna start my own handyman business, easy. Like, I was gonna show that building management company they made a huge mistake letting me go, you know? I can fix anything. Plumbing, drywall, whatever. Drywall is actually pretty tricky if you don't know how to tape the seams right. You gotta use the mesh tape, not that paper crap. The paper always bubbles up if you don't get the mud perfectly smooth underneath it. I learned that the hard way when I was trying to patch a hole in my buddy's living room. Someone threw a pool cue through the wall during a party. It wasn't even a real pool table, it was one of those cheap mini ones you put on top of a dining table. The felt on it was all torn up anyway. Reminded me of the felt on the card tables down at the community center where the old guys play pinochle all day. They take it so seriously, slamming the cards down and yelling at each other over pennies. I tried to learn how to play once, but there are too many rules to remember. I prefer games where you just roll the dice and see what happens, you know? Just let it ride. But yeah, I was totally ready to run my own business, I felt like I could do it in my sleep.

We tried getting it to turn over for like three days straight before we just gave up and sold it for parts to this guy who showed up with a flatbed trailer. The guy had this really mean-looking bulldog sitting in the passenger seat of his rig, just staring at me the whole time we were loading the car. Gave me the creeps, honestly. Dogs with those squished-in faces, you never know what they're thinking. They always sound like they're choking on something. But uh... wait, what did you ask me? Sleep, right? The smell from the river in the summer, man, it was awful. Like dead fish and old garbage cooking in the sun. They used to dump all kinds of chemicals in there back in the day, before all those environmental laws. My grandpa used to tell me stories about how the water would actually catch on fire sometimes. Can you imagine that? A river on fire. He was a fireman, actually, my grandpa. Had this big shiny brass helmet he kept on the mantle. I used to try it on when I was a kid, but it was way too heavy for my little head. Almost broke my nose once when it slipped down over my eyes. When I was a kid, I used to take apart the toaster just to see how the coils got hot. My mom got so mad at me 'cause I couldn't always put it back together right, and one time it sparked and blew a fuse in the kitchen. We had to eat cold cereal in the dark for like two hours until my dad got home from the mill to fix it. He worked at the paper mill, you know? The smell from that place was almost as bad as the river, smelled like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. He used to come home with his clothes smelling like it, and my mom would make him strip down on the back porch before he even came in the house. She was real strict about keeping the house clean. She used to scrub the linoleum floors on her hands and knees with a toothbrush. But yeah... I was telling people I was gonna run my own business and show 'em all. They thought I was crazy, but I knew I could do it.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, were you ever hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? Nah, no way. I never went to the hospital for any of that. I mean, hospitals give me the creeps anyway. The lighting in those places, it's always that harsh fluorescent buzzing stuff. Makes everyone look like they've been dead for a week. I remember when my buddy Ricky broke his leg dirt biking out by the old quarry, we had to wait in the ER for like six hours. The chairs were bolted to the floor and they were made of this hard blue plastic that just dug right into your spine. Ricky was whining the whole time, but honestly, the guy sitting next to us was worse. He kept coughing into this crumpled up tissue that looked like it had been in his pocket since 1998. He had on these boots, too—those big heavy steel-toed ones, but the leather was all cracked and peeling like old paint. Reminded me of the paint on my front porch, actually. I've been meaning to scrape that down and repaint it before the winter hits, but you know how it goes. You buy the scraper, you buy the sandpaper, and then it just sits in the garage gathering dust. My garage is a mess right now anyway. But uh, yeah, no hospital for me. Never had to go in for feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yeah, definitely. There's been times, a lot of times actually, where it goes on for weeks. Most days I just wake up with this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest, you know? Like there's just nothing good waiting for me out there, so I just want to keep my head down and not deal with any of it. It's like waking up and feeling like you're already behind on everything. Which reminds me, I still haven't paid that water bill from last month. They sent me this pink notice in the mail, but the mailman around here is terrible. Half the time he puts my mail in Mrs. Gable's box next door. She's got this yappy little dog, a Pomeranian or something, that barks at literally everything. A leaf blows across the street and that dog loses its mind. She leaves it out in the yard all day while she goes to her knitting club or whatever she does. My buddy Ricky used to have a dog, a big Rottweiler mix named Buster. Buster was a good boy, but he chewed through the drywall in Ricky's kitchen once. Just ate a hole right through to the living room. Ricky tried to patch it himself with some plaster he found in his dad's shed, but it looked terrible. Looked like a giant lumpy tumor on the wall. But yeah... that heavy feeling just kind of sits there for weeks sometimes, making it hard to even care about getting out of bed.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did your behavior cause serious problems at work, with money, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Serious problems? Nah, I wouldn't call it serious problems. I mean, sure, some people got a little annoyed with me, but they just didn't see the vision, you know? Like my buddy Ricky, he told me I was talking too fast and not making sense, but Ricky's always been slow on the uptake. I remember when we were trying to fix up his old Chevy Nova, the one with the rusted-out floorboards. We had to use old license plates to patch the holes so his feet wouldn't drag on the pavement. The guy at the junkyard charged us way too much for those plates, too. He had this glass eye that didn't quite move right, and he kept staring at me with his good eye while he counted the cash. I hate carrying cash, honestly. The coins always make my pockets heavy and they smell like dirty metal. My mom used to collect those state quarters when they first came out. She had this big cardboard map of the US and she'd pop the quarters in whenever she found a new one. I think she was only missing Hawaii when she gave up on it. But yeah, no serious problems, really. Just people not keeping up with me.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. I was going non-stop. I started calling up all these old tenants, making these huge lists, pricing out tools, and just trying to line up handyman work all day long. Way more than I ever did before, you know? I was looking at this one power drill online, a yellow and black one. Which is funny because it reminds me of those huge carpenter bees that used to get stuck in my grandma's screen door when I was a kid. We had this old wooden screen door that would slam so loud every time you let it go. Whack! Used to drive her absolutely crazy. She'd be in the kitchen making these little powdered sugar cookies... pecan sandies, I think she called them. They were so dry, man, you had to drink like a whole gallon of milk just to swallow one. Speaking of milk, they changed the caps on the plastic jugs at the corner store. They're attached to the plastic ring now so you can't drop them, but it just gets in the way when you try to pour it. Ends up splashing all over the counter and making a sticky mess. I hate sticky counters, you have to wipe them down like five times to get the residue off. But yeah, I was just making lists and pricing out tools all day.\n\nDoctor: Even when you are not feeling especially up or depressed, do people still tell you that you talk in a way that jumps around or is hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Nah, I don't think so. When I'm just feeling regular, nobody says anything about how I talk. They understand me just fine. Like, I was talking to the guy at the auto parts store the other day, just regular, asking about windshield wipers. The ones on my car are totally shot right now, they just smear the rain around instead of wiping it off. It's dangerous, honestly. Especially at night when the streetlights hit the glass. They changed all the streetlights on my block to those new LED ones, you know? They're so bright, man, it's like a spaceship beam coming right into my bedroom window. I had to put up aluminum foil just to get some sleep. My buddy Ricky did that once, but he used duct tape to hold it up and it left this awful sticky residue on the glass that he couldn't get off with anything. He tried scraping it with a razor blade and just scratched the whole window up. Looked terrible from the street. But yeah, no, usually people don't have a problem following me when I'm just normal.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did you spend much more money than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't really spend extra. I didn't have a lot of cash to throw around anyway, you know? I just keep what little I have in this old leather wallet I got at a flea market years ago. It's got one of those thick chains on it so nobody can swipe it. I like chains because I'm always losing my stuff, especially my keys. Man, just last week I spent like two hours looking for my house keys. Turned out they fell way down deep in the couch cushions. Have you ever actually pulled the cushions off and looked under there? It's crazy what gets stuck down in the cracks. I found this old remote control for a TV I haven't even owned since, like, 2015. It was one of those huge heavy box TVs that takes two guys to lift. When I moved out of my old place, Ricky helped me carry it down three flights of stairs and he almost dropped his end right on his toe. He was wearing these cheap rubber flip-flops, which is just a stupid idea when you're moving heavy furniture. I told him he should go down to the army surplus store and get some steel-toe boots. They have a whole wall of them, but the whole store smells like mothballs. I can't stand that smell, it gets stuck in your nose for the rest of the day and makes everything taste weird. But yeah, no, I wasn't buying a bunch of stuff or spending more money.\n\nDoctor: That time when you felt untouchable and needed only two or three hours of sleep—did that last for at least a week?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, man. It's hard to say if it was a whole week or what, because time just gets all fuzzy when you're going like that. Like everything is just moving fast and blurring together. Kind of like when you're on the highway and the white lines on the road just turn into one long streak, you know? My cousin used to drive this beat-up old Chevy Nova, and the speedometer was completely busted, so we never actually knew how fast we were going. We'd just guess based on how loud the engine was screaming. That car was a total piece of junk, honestly. The heater only blew cold air, which was brutal in the winter. We used to have to wear our heavy winter coats inside the car just to drive to the store. I had this big puffy jacket, bright orange, like the ones hunters wear so they don't get shot in the woods. I never went hunting, though. I don't think I could shoot a deer, they just look at you with those big eyes, kind of like my neighbor's dog. It's a boxer mix, I think. Barks at every single person that walks by the chain-link fence, just going crazy over absolutely nothing. Drives me up the wall when I'm trying to think. But yeah, I couldn't tell you if it was exactly a week or not. I wasn't really looking at a calendar or anything.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did you feel more important or more capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely, man. I was telling everyone I was gonna start my own handyman business, easy. Like, I was gonna show that building management company they made a huge mistake letting me go, you know? I can fix anything. Plumbing, drywall, whatever. Drywall is actually pretty tricky if you don't know how to tape the seams right. You gotta use the mesh tape, not that paper crap. The paper always bubbles up if you don't get the mud perfectly smooth underneath it. I learned that the hard way when I was trying to patch a hole in my buddy's living room. Someone threw a pool cue through the wall during a party. It wasn't even a real pool table, it was one of those cheap mini ones you put on top of a dining table. The felt on it was all torn up anyway. Reminded me of the felt on the card tables down at the community center where the old guys play pinochle all day. They take it so seriously, slamming the cards down and yelling at each other over pennies. I tried to learn how to play once, but there are too many rules to remember. I prefer games where you just roll the dice and see what happens, you know? Just let it ride. But yeah, I was totally ready to run my own business, I felt like I could do it in my sleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The smell from the river in the summer, man, it was awful. Like dead fish and old garbage cooking in the sun. They used to dump all kinds of chemicals in there back in the day, before all those environmental laws. My grandpa used to tell me stories about how the water would actually catch on fire sometimes. Makes everyone look like they've been dead for a week. I remember when my buddy Ricky broke his leg dirt biking out by the old quarry, we had to wait in the ER for like six hours. The chairs were bolted to the floor and they were made of this hard blue plastic that just dug right into your spine. Ricky was whining the whole time, but honestly, the guy sitting next to us was worse. He kept coughing into this crumpled up tissue that looked like it had been in his pocket since 1998. Serious trouble? I mean, not really, no. I was just doing my thing, you know? People just get so uptight about stuff. A full week? Nah, man, I don't think so. I mean, a week is a long time to go without sleep. Like, you'd start seeing things, right? Yeah, I guess so. I was calling my sister, trying to tell her about all this stuff—prices, flyers, how we could get tenants for this idea I had. Oh, yeah, definitely. I was going non-stop. I started calling up all these old tenants, making all these lists, pricing out tools, and trying to line up handyman work all day long. The smell from the river in the summer, man, it was awful. Like dead fish and old garbage cooking in the sun. They used to dump all kinds of chemicals in there back in the day, before all those environmental laws. My grandpa used to tell me stories about how the water would actually catch on fire sometimes. Can you imagine that? A river on fire. He was a fireman, actually, my grandpa. Had this big shiny brass helmet he kept on the mantle. I used to try it on when I was a kid, but it was way too heavy for my little head. Almost broke my nose once when it slipped down over my eyes. No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I was telling everyone I could start my own handyman business easy. Just like that, boom, start my own thing and show that packaging plant they made a huge mistake letting me go. I'm real good with my hands, always have been. When I was a kid, I used to take apart the toaster just to see how the coils got hot. My mom got so mad at me 'cause I couldn't always put it back together right, and one time it sparked and blew a fuse in the kitchen. We had to eat cold cereal in the dark for like two hours until my dad got home from the mill to fix it. He worked at the paper mill, you know? The smell from that place was almost as bad as the river, smelled like rotten eggs and boiled cabbage. He used to come home with his clothes smelling like it, and my mom would make him strip down on the back porch before he even came in the house. She was real strict about keeping the house clean. She used to scrub the linoleum floors on her hands and knees with a toothbrush. But yeah... I was telling people I was gonna run my own business and show 'em all. I never went to the hospital for any of that. I mean, hospitals give me the creeps anyway. The lighting in those places, it's always that harsh fluorescent buzzing stuff. Makes everyone look like they've been dead for a week. I remember when my buddy Ricky broke his leg dirt biking out by the old quarry, we had to wait in the ER for like six hours. The chairs were bolted to the floor and they were made of this hard blue plastic that just dug right into your spine. Ricky was whining the whole time, but honestly, the guy sitting next to us was worse. He kept coughing into this crumpled up tissue that looked like it had been in his pocket since 1998. He had on these boots, too—those big heavy steel-toed ones, but the leather was all cracked and peeling like old paint. Reminded me of the paint on my front porch, actually. I've been meaning to scrape that down and repaint it before the winter hits, but you know how it goes. You buy the scraper, you buy the sandpaper, and then it just sits in the garage gathering dust. My garage is a mess right now anyway. But uh, yeah, no hospital for me. Like there's just nothing good waiting for me out there, so I just want to keep my head down and not deal with any of it. It's like waking up and feeling like you're already behind on everything. Which reminds me, I still haven't paid that water bill from last month. They sent me this pink notice in the mail, but the mailman around here is terrible. Half the time he puts my mail in Mrs. Gable's box next door. She's got this yappy little dog, a Pomeranian or something, that barks at literally everything. A leaf blows across the street and that dog loses its mind. She leaves it out in the yard all day while she goes to her knitting club or whatever she does. Just ate a hole right through to the living room. Ricky tried to patch it himself with some plaster he found in his dad's shed, but it looked terrible. Looked like a giant lumpy tumor on the wall. Nah, I wouldn't call it serious problems. I mean, sure, some people got a little annoyed with me, but they just didn't see the vision, you know? Like my buddy Ricky, he told me I was talking too fast and not making sense, but Ricky's always been slow on the uptake. I remember when we were trying to fix up his old Chevy Nova, the one with the rusted-out floorboards. We had to use old license plates to patch the holes so his feet wouldn't drag on the pavement. The guy at the junkyard charged us way too much for those plates, too. He had this glass eye that didn't quite move right, and he kept staring at me with his good eye while he counted the cash. I hate carrying cash, honestly. The coins always make my pockets heavy and they smell like dirty metal. My mom used to collect those state quarters when they first came out. She had this big cardboard map of the US and she'd pop the quarters in whenever she found a new one. I think she was only missing Hawaii when she gave up on it. But yeah, no serious problems, really. Just people not keeping up with me.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. Way more than I ever did before, you know? I was looking at this one power drill online, a yellow and black one. Which is funny because it reminds me of those huge carpenter bees that used to get stuck in my grandma's screen door when I was a kid. We had this old wooden screen door that would slam so loud every time you let it go. Whack! Used to drive her absolutely crazy. She'd be in the kitchen making these little powdered sugar cookies... pecan sandies, I think she called them. They were so dry, man, you had to drink like a whole gallon of milk just to swallow one. Speaking of milk, they changed the caps on the plastic jugs at the corner store. They're attached to the plastic ring now so you can't drop them, but it just gets in the way when you try to pour it. Ends up splashing all over the counter and making a sticky mess. I hate sticky counters, you have to wipe them down like five times to get the residue off. But yeah, I was just making lists and pricing out tools all day.\n\nDoctor: Even when you are not feeling especially up or depressed, do people still tell you that you talk in a way that jumps around or is hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Nah, I don't think so. When I'm just feeling regular, nobody says anything about how I talk. They understand me just fine. Like, I was talking to the guy at the auto parts store the other day, just regular, asking about windshield wipers. The ones on my car are totally shot right now, they just smear the rain around instead of wiping it off. It's dangerous, honestly. Especially at night when the streetlights hit the glass. They changed all the streetlights on my block to those new LED ones, you know? They're so bright, man, it's like a spaceship beam coming right into my bedroom window. I had to put up aluminum foil just to get some sleep. My buddy Ricky did that once, but he used duct tape to hold it up and it left this awful sticky residue on the glass that he couldn't get off with anything. He tried scraping it with a razor blade and just scratched the whole window up. Looked terrible from the street. I didn't have a lot of cash to throw around anyway, you know? I just keep what little I have in this old leather wallet I got at a flea market years ago. It's got one of those thick chains on it so nobody can swipe it. I like chains because I'm always losing my stuff, especially my keys. Man, just last week I spent like two hours looking for my house keys. Turned out they fell way down deep in the couch cushions. Have you ever actually pulled the cushions off and looked under there? It's crazy what gets stuck down in the cracks. I found this old remote control for a TV I haven't even owned since, like, 2015. It was one of those huge heavy box TVs that takes two guys to lift. When I moved out of my old place, Ricky helped me carry it down three flights of stairs and he almost dropped his end right on his toe. He was wearing these cheap rubber flip-flops, which is just a stupid idea when you're moving heavy furniture. I told him he should go down to the army surplus store and get some steel-toe boots. They have a whole wall of them, but the whole store smells like mothballs. I can't stand that smell, it gets stuck in your nose for the rest of the day and makes everything taste weird. But yeah, no, I wasn't buying a bunch of stuff or spending more money.\n\nDoctor: That time when you felt untouchable and needed only two or three hours of sleep—did that last for at least a week?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, man. It's hard to say if it was a whole week or what, because time just gets all fuzzy when you're going like that. Like everything is just moving fast and blurring together. Kind of like when you're on the highway and the white lines on the road just turn into one long streak, you know? My cousin used to drive this beat-up old Chevy Nova, and the speedometer was completely busted, so we never actually knew how fast we were going. We'd just guess based on how loud the engine was screaming. That car was a total piece of junk, honestly. The heater only blew cold air, which was brutal in the winter. We used to have to wear our heavy winter coats inside the car just to drive to the store. I had this big puffy jacket, bright orange, like the ones hunters wear so they don't get shot in the woods. I never went hunting, though. I don't think I could shoot a deer, they just look at you with those big eyes, kind of like my neighbor's dog. It's a boxer mix, I think. Barks at every single person that walks by the chain-link fence, just going crazy over absolutely nothing. Drives me up the wall when I'm trying to think. But yeah, I couldn't tell you if it was exactly a week or not. I wasn't really looking at a calendar or anything.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, did you feel more important or more capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely, man. I was telling everyone I was gonna start my own handyman business, easy. Like, I was gonna show that building management company they made a huge mistake letting me go, you know? I can fix anything. Plumbing, drywall, whatever. Drywall is actually pretty tricky if you don't know how to tape the seams right. You gotta use the mesh tape, not that paper crap. The paper always bubbles up if you don't get the mud perfectly smooth underneath it. I learned that the hard way when I was trying to patch a hole in my buddy's living room. Someone threw a pool cue through the wall during a party. It wasn't even a real pool table, it was one of those cheap mini ones you put on top of a dining table. The felt on it was all torn up anyway. Reminded me of the felt on the card tables down at the community center where the old guys play pinochle all day. They take it so seriously, slamming the cards down and yelling at each other over pennies. I tried to learn how to play once, but there are too many rules to remember. I prefer games where you just roll the dice and see what happens, you know? Just let it ride. But yeah, I was totally ready to run my own business, I felt like I could do it in my sleep.\n\nDoctor: Did that period when you felt fired up and needed very little sleep last at least four days in a row?\n\nPatient: Four days? I'd just be sitting there on the couch watching those late-night infomercials. Have you seen the one for that super blender? The guy puts whole pineapples and, like, a handful of rocks in there just to show how strong the blades are. I actually bought one of those once, back when I was working at the warehouse. Cost me three easy payments of thirty-nine ninety-nine. But the thing was so loud it sounded like a lawnmower starting up in my kitchen. He's this grumpy old dude who used to be a mechanic, always wearing those blue coveralls with the grease stains on the knees. The guy never leaves the building except to smoke those cheap little cigars that smell like burnt plastic. But anyway, yeah, I couldn't tell you if it was four days straight or not. It was just a bunch of time.\n\nDoctor: During that fired-up period, did your behavior ever get you into serious trouble with other people, work, or the law?\n\nPatient: Serious trouble? I mean, not really, no. I was just doing my thing, you know? People just get so uptight about stuff. Like, I was trying to explain my business idea to this guy at the diner, just sketching out the logo on a napkin, and he got all weird about me talking to him. The napkins there are so cheap, by the way, they just tear the second you get them wet. It's that thin, scratchy paper. Reminds me of the toilet paper they had at my middle school. We used to wet it and throw it at the ceiling in the boys' bathroom. It would stick up there like these little gray spitballs and just harden like cement. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he'd get so mad. He had this big ring of keys that jingled from a mile away, so you always knew when he was coming down the hall. He drove this ancient station wagon with wood paneling on the side. I always wanted a car with wood paneling, it just looks classy, you know? Like you're going on a road trip across the country. I've never been out west, but I've seen pictures of the Grand Canyon. It's just a big hole in the ground, really, if you think about it. But yeah, no cops or anything like that. I didn't get into any real trouble, people just don't appreciate a guy with vision.\n\nDoctor: At any point in your life, has there ever been a time when feeling that fired up and needing very little sleep lasted a full week or longer?\n\nPatient: A full week? Nah, man, I don't think so. I mean, a week is a long time to go without sleep. Like, you'd start seeing things, right? I watched this documentary once about this radio DJ back in the fifties or sixties, I think. He stayed awake for, like, over two hundred hours as a publicity stunt. He was broadcasting from a glass booth in Times Square. Can you imagine everyone just staring at you while you're trying to stay awake? People in New York are crazy anyway, they just walk around at all hours. I went there once when I was a kid, we took a bus trip. The bus smelled like stale coffee and that blue chemical stuff they put in the toilet in the back. My sister got motion sickness the whole way there. She always gets sick in cars unless she's sitting in the front seat. She tried those little wristbands with the plastic bump that presses on your vein, but they didn't do anything. I think it's all in her head, honestly. If you just look out the window at the horizon, you're fine. The horizon is always straight, even when the road is bumpy. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, a week. No, definitely not a whole week. I'd crash way before that.\n\nDoctor: During that fired-up time, were you talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I was calling my sister, trying to tell her about all this stuff—prices, flyers, how we could get tenants for this idea I had. She said I was talking so fast she couldn't even get a word in edgewise. She's always complaining about something, though. Like, last week she called me just to complain about her neighbor's dog. It's this little yappy thing, a Pomeranian or something. Looks like a giant cotton ball with legs. I don't get why people buy dogs that small. What's the point? You can't play fetch with it, it'd probably choke on a tennis ball. My uncle had a real dog, a German Shepherd named Buster. Buster was huge, he used to eat whole chickens, bones and all. My uncle would just throw the whole raw chicken out in the yard and Buster would go to town. He lived out in the country, my uncle did. Had this big old farmhouse with a wraparound porch. The wood on the porch was all splintered and gray because he never painted it. He said he liked the \"weathered look,\" but I think he was just lazy. He spent most of his time sitting in this rusted old lawn chair, drinking cheap beer out of a can. The cans always had those pop-tops that you pull off entirely, you know? You don't see those anymore. People used to make chains out of them, like long silver necklaces. I tried making one once, but I cut my finger on the sharp edge. Bled everywhere. Had to wrap it in a paper towel and put duct tape over it. Duct tape fixes everything, man. But yeah, my sister said I was talking too fast. I just had a lot of ideas to get out, you know?\n\nDoctor: During that fired-up time, were you doing a lot more activities or starting more projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. Says he lost it in a band saw accident or something. You gotta be careful with power tools. My buddy Rick bought a chainsaw at a yard sale once, and the chain just snapped right off the first time he used it. It whipped around and took a huge chunk out of his favorite jacket. It was one of those old denim jackets with the thick fleece collar, you know? They really don't make them like that anymore. Now everything is just cheap polyester that melts if you get too close to a campfire. I love a good campfire, though. The smell of woodsmoke just sticks in your hair for days, no matter how much shampoo you use. I usually just buy the cheap stuff, the three-in-one kind, because who has time for conditioner? It all goes down the same drain anyway. Yeah, it was right after I got fired. I remember walking out of there feeling... I don't know, fired up. Like I was untouchable, you know? Like they did me a favor. I didn't need them anyway. The manager there, he always wore these cheap polyester ties that looked like they were from the seventies. Brown and orange stripes, who wears that? And he always smelled like stale onions. I think he ate those frozen onion rings for lunch every single day. You know the ones that come in the cardboard box and you bake them, but they never get crispy? They just get kind of soggy and sad. I used to work at a diner where we made real onion rings, from scratch. You gotta soak the onions in buttermilk first, that's the secret. And use a light batter, not that thick heavy stuff. The fry cook there, his name was Sal. Big guy, always sweating. He had this tattoo of a mermaid on his forearm, but it was really faded so it just looked like a green blob. He told me he got it when he was in the Navy. I thought about joining the Navy once, just to see the ocean. I've never been to the ocean. I bet it smells like salt and dead fish, but in a good way, you know? But yeah, I felt untouchable right after getting fired. Though, I was snapping at people pretty fast if they told me to slow down or take it easy. Like, just get out of my way, I got things to do.
